\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}:(\mathbf{U}_{sm}\to\mathbf{U}_{sm})\rightharpoonup\mathbf{U}_{sm}
; and

•

A function
\mathord{\underline{\forall}}:(\mathbf{U}_{sm}\to\mathbf{U}_{lg})\rightharpoonup\mathbf{U}_{lg}
.

Let
\mathrm{TyVar}
be the set of type variables and
let
\eta:\mathrm{TyVar}\to\mathbf{U}_{sm}
. The denotation of
\tau
with respect
to
\eta
,
{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{\eta}
, is given in
Figure
18
.

Definition 6
.

A type frame is a
type model
if for all
F
and
\eta
,

{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}V.{}\llbracket{F}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{t}\mapsto{V}\}}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\nolimits\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
and for all
\tau

and all
\eta
,
{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}U.{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{\alpha}\mapsto{U}\}}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\nolimits\mathord{\underline{\forall}}

(and hence
{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{\eta}
is defined for all
\tau
and
\eta
).

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {}\llbracket{\alpha}\rrbracket{\eta}&=\eta(\alpha)\\
{}\llbracket{\mathsf{unit}}\rrbracket{\eta}&=U_{\mathsf{unit}}\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} {}\llbracket{{\sigma}\times{\sigma^{\prime}}}\rrbracket{\eta}&={}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket{\eta}\mathrel{\underline{\times}}{}\llbracket{\sigma^{\prime}}\rrbracket{\eta}\\
{}\llbracket{{\sigma}+{\sigma^{\prime}}}\rrbracket{\eta}&={}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket{\eta}\mathrel{\underline{+}}{}\llbracket{\sigma^{\prime}}\rrbracket{\eta}\\
{}\llbracket{{\sigma}\to{\sigma^{\prime}}}\rrbracket{\eta}&={}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket{\eta}\mathrel{\underline{\mathord{\to}}}{}\llbracket{\sigma^{\prime}}\rrbracket{\eta}\\
{}\llbracket{\mu{t}.F}\rrbracket{\eta}&=\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}V.{}\llbracket{F}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{t}\mapsto{V}\}}})\end{aligned}

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\forall\alpha.\tau}\rrbracket{\eta}=\mathord{\underline{\forall}}({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}U.{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{\alpha}\mapsto{U}\}}})

Figure 18
.
The denotation (partial) function of types and type schemes
into a type frame.

Definition 7
.

An
applicative structure
is specified by the following data:

•

A type model
(\mathbf{U}_{sm},\mathbf{U}_{lg})
.

•

For each
U\in\mathbf{U}_{lg}
, a preordered set
(D^{U},\leq_{U})
.

•

For
each
\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\nolimits\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
and
U,V\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}
, a function

\Phi_{U,V}:(D^{U}\to D^{V})\to(D^{\Phi\,U}\to D^{\Phi\,V})
.

•

Distinguished elements
0,1\in D^{U_{\mathsf{C}}}
and an associative function

+:D^{U_{\mathsf{C}}}\to D^{U_{\mathsf{C}}}
such that
0
is a right- and left identity for
+
.

•

A distinguished element
*\in D^{U_{\mathsf{unit}}}

•

For each
U,V\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}
, functions

(D^{U}\to D^{V})\xrightharpoonup{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Abs}}}_{U,V}}D^{U\mathrel{\underline{\mathord{\to}}}V}\xrightarrow{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{App}}}_{U,V}}(D^{U}\to D^{V})

such that

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{App}}}\mathbin{\circ}\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Abs}}}\geq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
. Note that
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Abs}}}
is a partial function.

•

For each
U_{0},U_{1}\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}
, functions

D^{U_{0}}\times D^{U_{1}}\xrightarrow{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Pair}}}_{U_{0},U_{1}}}D^{U_{0}\mathrel{\underline{\times}}U_{1}}\xrightarrow{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Proj}}}_{U_{0},U_{1}}^{i}}D^{U_{i}}

such that

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Proj}}}^{i}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Pair}}}(a_{0},a_{1}))\geq a_{i}
.

•

For each
U_{0}
,
U_{1}
,
V\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}
, functions

D^{U_{i}}\xrightarrow{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}_{U_{0},U_{1}}^{i}}D^{U_{0}\mathrel{\underline{+}}U_{1}}\xrightarrow{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}_{U_{0},U_{1},V}}(D^{U_{0}}\to V)\times(D^{U_{1}}\to V)\to D^{V}

such that

(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}\mathbin{\circ}\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{i})\,a\,(f_{0},f_{1})\geq f_{i}\,a
.
We often write
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}(a,f_{0},f_{1})
for
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}\,a\,(f_{0},f_{1})
.

•

For each
\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\nolimits\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
, functions

D^{\Phi(\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi)}\xrightarrow{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{\Phi}}D^{\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi}\xrightarrow{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{\Phi}}D^{\Phi(\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi)}

such that

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}\mathbin{\circ}\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}\geq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
.

•

For each
\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\nolimits\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
and
U\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}
, functions

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}:(D^{\Phi\,U}\to D^{U})\to(D^{\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi}\to D^{U})

such that

(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}\,f)\mathbin{\circ}\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{\Phi}\geq f\mathbin{\circ}(\Phi_{\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi,U}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}\,f))
.

•

For each
\Phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\nolimits\forall
, functions

\prod_{U\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}}D^{\Phi(U)}\xrightharpoonup{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyAbs}}}_{\Phi}}D^{\mathord{\underline{\forall}}(\Phi)}\xrightarrow{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyApp}}}_{\Phi}}\prod_{U\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}}D^{\Phi(U)}

such that
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyApp}}}\mathbin{\circ}\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyAbs}}})\geq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
. Note that
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyAbs}}}
is a partial
function.

Remember that when we write, e.g.,
D^{U}\to D^{V}
, we mean the monotone
functions from
D^{U}
to
D^{V}
, and hence all of the semantic functions that
make up the data of an applicative structure are monotone.

We write
{\mathbf{U}}=(\mathbf{U}_{sm},\mathbf{U}_{lg},\{D^{U}\}_{{U\in\mathbf{U}_{lg}}})
for a typical
applicative structure, or just
{\mathbf{U}}=\{D^{U}\}_{{U\in\mathbf{U}_{lg}}}
when

\mathbf{U}_{lg}
is clear from context.
For a context
\Gamma
define

\mathrm{tyvar}(\Gamma)=\{\alpha\mid\text{$\alpha$ occurs in
$\mathrm{ftv}(\Gamma(x))$ for some~$x$}\}
.
Define
a
\Gamma
-environment
to be a map
\eta
such that

•

\eta(\alpha)\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}
for
\alpha\in\mathrm{tyvar}(\Gamma)
; and

•

\eta(x)\in D^{{}\llbracket{\Gamma(x)}\rrbracket{\eta}}
for
x\in\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\nolimits\Gamma
.

For an applicative structure and environment
\eta
, define
a partial denotation function
{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{e}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}
as in
Figure
19
. The only way in which

{}\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket{\cdot}
may fail to be total is if the arguments
to
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Abs}}}
or
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyAbs}}}
are not in the corresponding domains (because we
start with a type model, we know that
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
and
\mathord{\underline{\forall}}
are only applied
to functions in their domains).

Definition 8
.

Let
{\mathbf{U}}
be an applicative structure.

(1)

{\mathbf{U}}
is a
pre-model
if

•

Whenever
{\Gamma}\vdash{e}\mathbin{:}{\tau}
and
\eta
is a
\Gamma
-environment,

{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{e}\mathbin{:}{\tau}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}
is defined and an element
of
D^{{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{\eta}}
; and

•

Whenever
{\Gamma,y\mathbin{:}\rho}\vdash{e^{\prime}}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}
and

{\Gamma}\vdash{e}\mathbin{:}{{F}[{\rho}]}
and
\eta
is a
\Gamma
-environment,

{}\llbracket{\mbox{${F}[({y}:{\rho}).{e^{\prime}},{e}]$}}\rrbracket{\eta}\leq_{{}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket{\eta}}({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}V.{}\llbracket{F}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{t}\mapsto{V}\}}})_{{}\llbracket{\rho}\rrbracket{\eta},{}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket{\eta}}({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}a.{}\llbracket{e^{\prime}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{y}\mapsto{a}\}}})({}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta}).

(2)

{\mathbf{U}}
is a
model
if
{\mathbf{U}}
is a pre-model and whenever

{\Gamma}\vdash{e}\leq_{\tau}{e^{\prime}}
, and
\eta
is a
\Gamma
-environment,

{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{e}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}\leq_{{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{\eta}}{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{e^{\prime}}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}
.

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma,x\mathbin{:}\tau}\vdash{x}\mathbin{:}{\tau}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\eta(x)

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{(\,)}\mathbin{:}{\mathsf{unit}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\mathord{*}

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{(e_{0},e_{1})}\mathbin{:}{{\sigma_{0}}\times{\sigma_{1}}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Pair}}}({}\llbracket{e_{0}}\rrbracket{\eta},{}\llbracket{e_{1}}\rrbracket{\eta})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{\pi_{i}\,e}\mathbin{:}{\sigma_{i}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Proj}}}^{i}({}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{\iota_{i}\,e}\mathbin{:}{{\sigma_{0}}+{\sigma_{1}}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{i}({}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{case}\,e\,\mathsf{of}\,{x}.{e_{0}};{x}.{e_{1}}}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}({}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta},{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}a.{}\llbracket{e_{0}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{a}\}}},{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}a.{}\llbracket{e_{1}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{a}\}}})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{\lambda{x}.{e}}\mathbin{:}{{\sigma}\to{\sigma^{\prime}}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Abs}}}({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}a.{}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{a}\}}})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{{e}\,{e^{\prime}}}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{App}}}({}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta})\,({}\llbracket{e^{\prime}}\rrbracket{\eta})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{c}_{\delta}\,{e}}\mathbin{:}{\delta}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{{\lambda\hskip-3.68748pt\lambda}V.{}\llbracket{F}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{t}\mapsto{V}\}}}}({}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{d}_{\delta}\,{e}}\mathbin{:}{{F}[{\delta}]}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{{\lambda\hskip-3.68748pt\lambda}V.{}\llbracket{F}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{t}\mapsto{V}\}}}}({}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{fold}_{\delta}\,e^{\prime}\,\mathsf{of}\,{x}.{e}}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{{\lambda\hskip-3.68748pt\lambda}V.{}\llbracket{F}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{t}\mapsto{V}\}}},{}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket{\eta}}({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}a.{}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{a}\}}})\,({}\llbracket{e^{\prime}}\rrbracket{\eta})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{\Lambda{\alpha}.{e}}\mathbin{:}{\forall{\alpha}.\tau}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyAbs}}}_{{\lambda\hskip-3.68748pt\lambda}U.{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{\alpha}\mapsto{U}\}}}}({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}U.{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{e}\mathbin{:}{\tau}$}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{\alpha}\mapsto{U}\}}})

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{{e}\,{\sigma}}\mathbin{:}{{\tau}{\{{\sigma}/{\alpha}\}}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyApp}}}_{{\lambda\hskip-3.68748pt\lambda}U.{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{\alpha}\mapsto{U}\}}}}({}\llbracket{\mbox{${\Gamma}\vdash{e}\mathbin{:}{\forall{\alpha}.\tau}$}}\rrbracket{\eta})({}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket{\eta})

Figure 19
.
The denotation (partial) function into an applicative
structure. For constructors and destructors, assume
\delta=\mu{t}.F

and
\mathop{\mathrm{fv}}\nolimits(\delta)=\{\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}\}
, and define

\eta^{*}={\eta}{\{{\vec{\alpha}}\mapsto{\vec{U}}\}}
.

The indirection of interpreting syntactic
types by semantic types, and then
interpreting terms of a given syntactic type as elements of a domain associated
to the corresponding semantic type is necessary, especially in our setting
of non-standard models. This makes is much easier (seemingly,

possible
) to define things like the
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
operator. Without
the indirection, we would have to define
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
on (functions on)
a collection of domains, some of which represent syntactic types. That
ends up being very difficult to do. For example, we might have to first
define a notion of polynomial function on the semantic domains in order
to define the domain of
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
, and then somehow identify each semantic
polynomial function with a structure functor. But doing so gets us into
problems with unique representation; e.g., there may be multiple
structure functors corresponding to the same semantic polynomial.
And with non-standard models, we seem to have even more troubles, because we
end up trying to define the interpretations of inductive types simultaneously
with the
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
function.
But first interpreting the syntactic types by semantic types gives us a way
around these problems, because (if we wish) we can define the semantic types
to be closely tied to the syntactic types. That is exactly what we do for the
standard type frame, so we can essentially define
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
syntactically,
and then choose a domain corresponding to
\mu{t}.F
(which is a
semantic type as well as a syntactic one) after having
defined
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{U}}
be a pre-model. Then:

(1)

{}\llbracket{{\tau}{\{{\sigma}/{\alpha}\}}}\rrbracket{\eta}={}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{\alpha}\mapsto{{}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket{\eta}}\}}}
. If
\alpha\notin\mathrm{ftv}(\tau)

then for all
U
,
{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{\eta}={}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{\alpha}\mapsto{U}\}}}
and
for all term variables
x
and all
a
,

{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{\eta}={}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{a}\}}}
.

(2)

{}\llbracket{{e}{\{{e^{\prime}}/{x}\}}}\rrbracket{\eta}={}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{{}\llbracket{e^{\prime}}\rrbracket{\eta}}\}}}
. If
x\notin\mathop{\mathrm{fv}}\nolimits(e)
, then
for all
a
,
{}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta}={}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{a}\}}}
.

(3)

If
a\leq a^{\prime}
, then

{}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{a}\}}}\leq{}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{a^{\prime}}\}}}
; in other words,

{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}a.{}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x}\mapsto{a}\}}}
is monotone.

Proposition 9

(Environment model soundness)
.

If
{\mathbf{U}}
is an pre-model, then
{\mathbf{U}}
is a model.

Proof 6.2
.

By induction on the derivation of
{\Gamma}\vdash{e}\leq_{\tau}{e^{\prime}}
.

One might hope that a model of the fragment of the recurrence language that
omits
\mathsf{fold}_{\delta}
can be extended to one that does, but in our setting
this does not quite hold.
Since we only have
directed versions
of the usual equalities, initial algebras for structure functors may not
exist. And even if they do, they are not necessarily what we want.
For clarity, in this discussion we will write syntactic types for semantic
types.
The point behind different semantics is to abstract inductive values to some
notion of size, and when this abstraction is non-trivial,

D^{\mu{t}.F}
and
D^{{F}[{\mu{t}.F}]}
are probably not isomorphic.
Instead of the usual initial algebra for
interpreting
\mu{t}.F
, we typically want an algebra

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}:D^{F[\mu{t}.F]}\to D^{\mu{t}.F}
such that for any
other algebra
s:D^{F[\sigma]}\to D^{\sigma}
, there is a
function
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{F,\sigma}\,s

that makes the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{F[\mu{t}.F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\mu{t}.F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{F[\sigma]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Map}}}_{F}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{F}\,s)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{F}\,s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

commute, where
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Map}}}_{F}
is a semantic function that corresponds to
the
F[\cdot,\cdot]
macro.
Relative to the usual definition of initial algebra, this requirement is

weak
, in that we ask only for existence of a
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}{}
function
making the diagram commute (
\beta
reduction) and not the uniqueness of

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}{}
(
\eta
/induction), and it is
lax
, in that we ask
that
\beta
-reduction holds only as an inequality, rather than an
equality.

Nonetheless, under assumptions that turn out to be relatively easy to ensure,
we can define
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}
. Given a subset
X\subseteq A
of a
preordered set
A
, we say that
a\in A
is a
least upper bound
of
X
,
written
a=\bigvee X
, if for all
x\in X
,
x\leq a
, and if
b\in A

satisfies the condition that for all
x\in X
,
x\leq b
, then
a\leq b
.
When
A
is preordered,
\bigvee X
may not exist, and when it does, it need
not be unique. If
A
is a
partial
order (i.e.,
a\leq b\leq a

implies that
a=b
), then
\bigvee X
is unique when it exists, and we say
that
A
is a
complete upper semi-lattice
if
A
is a partial order and

\bigvee X
exists for every
X\subseteq A
. Though this seems like a very
strong condition, in practice it is easy to ensure.

In a model in which every
D^{U}
is a complete upper semi-lattice, we would
like to define

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}\,s\,x=\bigvee\{s\bigl(\Phi_{\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi,U}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}s)\,z\bigr)\mid z\in D^{\Phi(\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi)},{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{\Phi}\,z\leq x}\}.

A priori
, this definition may not be well-founded, but in fact it is, as
shown in the next proposition.

Proposition 10
.

Suppose that
{\mathbf{U}}=\{D^{U}\}_{{U\in\mathbf{U}_{lg}}}
is a model
of the fragment of the recurrence language that omits
\mathsf{fold}_{\delta}

and each
D^{U}
is a complete upper semi-lattice, and suppose that

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}
is defined by (
6.1
). Then:

(1)

For all
s
,
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}\,s
is total and monotone.

(2)

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}
is total and monotone.

Proof 6.3
.

(1)

Fix
s
and consider

Q={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}g.{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}x.\bigvee\{s\bigl(\Phi_{\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi,U}\,g\,z\bigr)\mid z\in D^{\Phi(\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi)},\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{\Phi}\,z\leq x\}.

Q:(D^{\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi}\to D^{U})\to(D^{\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi}\to D^{U})
and it is easy to
see that
Q
is monotone.
Since
D^{U}
is a complete upper semi-lattice,

D^{\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi}\to D^{U}
is a complete
partial order. So

Q
has a least fixed point; that is
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}\,s
.
4
4

4

The least fixed
point is obtained by the standard iteration of
Q
starting at the bottom
element. Because we only have that
Q
is monotone (not necessarily
continuous on chains), the iteration may have to
be extended transfinitely—see

Davey and Priestley 1999
.

Monotonicity
of
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{\Phi,U}\,s
is immediate from its definition.

(2)

Totality follows from (1) and monotonicity
from the fact that the
function that maps a monotone function to its least fixed point is itself
monotone.

The proof of Prop.
10
, and hence the
interpretation of
\mathsf{fold}_{\delta}
, may seem a bit heavy-handed, making
use of general least fixed point theorems and even iterating into the
transfinite. As we noted earlier, we are in a setting in which we do not have
(and do not want) initial algebras, but must nonetheless show an
initiality-like property of a given algebra. Accordingly, we would expect to
use technology at least as strong as that needed for typical initial algebra
existence theorems. The canonical such theorem
(e.g., as described
by
Aczel 1988
) verifies that the least
fixed point of a set-continuous operator is an initial algebra,
and the verification consists of constructing the equivalent of

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}\,s
by induction on the (ordinal-indexed) construction of the least
fixed point.

The reader may have noticed that an
alternative possible definition for
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}
is

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}\,s\,x=s(\Phi(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}\,s)(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}\,x))

and Prop.
10
would still hold.
This fact witnesses that the initiality condition for

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{\Phi}:D^{\Phi(\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi)}\to D^{\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}\,\Phi}
is weak, in that
functions to other algebras are not unique.
In practice, this alternative definition
yields far worse bounds for extracted recurrences, because we end up defining

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{\Phi}\,x=\bigvee\{z\mid\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{\Phi}(z)\leq x\}
and so

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}\,s\,x=s(\Phi(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}\,s)(\bigvee\{z\mid\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{\Phi}(z)\leq x\}))
.
Monotonicity of
f
only allows us to conclude
that
f(\bigvee X)\geq\bigvee\{f(x)\mid x\in X\}
, but this putative definition
for
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}\,s
exposes a case in which this inequality is strict.

6.2.
The standard type frame

Our last step in our general discussion of models is to define the type frame
upon which all of our examples will be based. It gives us enough data to
provide a standard definition of the functions
\Phi_{U,V}
, which in turn
lets us use (
6.1
) to define
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}
and so for most
of our examples, it will suffice to define
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{\Phi}
(because we will
set
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{\Phi}=\bigvee\{z\mid\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{\Phi}(z)\leq x\}
).
Our examples are all based on variations of
the
standard type frame
, which is defined as follows:

•

\mathbf{U}_{sm}
is the set of closed types and
\mathbf{U}_{lg}
the set of closed type
schemes of the recurrence language.

•

\mathrel{\underline{\mathord{\to}}}
,
\mathrel{\underline{\times}}
, and
\mathrel{\underline{+}}
are the standard type constructors;
e.g.,
\sigma_{0}\mathrel{\underline{+}}\sigma_{1}={\sigma_{0}}+{\sigma_{1}}
.

•

\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\nolimits\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}=\{{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\sigma.F[\sigma]\mid\mathop{\mathrm{fv}}\nolimits(F)\subseteq\{t\}\}

and
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\sigma.F[\sigma])=\mu{t}.F
(we call a structure
functor
F
with
\mathop{\mathrm{fv}}\nolimits(F)\subseteq\{t\}

closed
).

•

\mathop{\mathrm{dom}}\nolimits\mathord{\underline{\forall}}=\{{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\sigma.{\tau}{\{{\sigma}/{\alpha}\}}\mid\mathop{\mathrm{fv}}\nolimits(\tau)=\{\alpha\}\}
and

\mathord{\underline{\forall}}({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\sigma.{\tau}{\{{\sigma}/{\alpha}\}})=\forall{\alpha}.\tau
.

It is straightforward to show that if
{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\sigma.F[\sigma]={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\sigma.F^{\prime}[\sigma]
, then
F=F^{\prime}
, and if

{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\sigma.{\tau}{\{{\sigma}/{\alpha}\}}={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\sigma.{\tau^{\prime}}{\{{\sigma}/{\alpha}\}}
, then
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
, so
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
and
\mathord{\underline{\forall}}
are well-defined.
It should be clear and occasionally helpful to observe that for any

\tau
and
environment
\eta=\{{\alpha_{0}}\mapsto{\sigma_{0}},\dots,{\alpha_{n-1}}\mapsto{\sigma_{n-1}}\}
,

{}\llbracket{\tau}\rrbracket{\eta}={\tau}{\{{\vec{\sigma}}/{\vec{\alpha}}\}}
.
For models based on the standard type frame and any closed structure
functor
F
, we will usually
write
F
in place of

{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\sigma.{F}[{\sigma}]
in subscripts for readability.

Proposition 11
.

The standard type frame is a type model.

For any applicative structure based on (an extension of) the standard type
frame, define

F_{\rho,\sigma}:(D^{\rho}\to D^{\sigma})\to(D^{{F}[{\rho}]}\to D^{{F}[{\sigma}]})
for each closed structure
functor
F
and closed
\rho
and
\sigma
by:

\begin{aligned} t_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,x&=g\,x\\
(\sigma_{0})_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,x&=x\\
\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} ({F_{0}}+{F_{1}})_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,x&=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}(x,{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}y.\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{0}((F_{0})_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,y),{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}y.\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{1}((F_{1})_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,y))\\
({F_{0}}\times{F_{1}})_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,x&=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Pair}}}((F_{0})_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Proj}}}^{0}x),(F_{1})_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Proj}}}^{1}x)),\\
({\sigma_{0}}\to{F})_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,x&={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}y.F_{\rho,\sigma}\,g\,(x\,y)\end{aligned}

Lemma 6.4
.

If
{\mathbf{U}}
is an applicative structure based on an extension of the standard type
frame that is a model for the fragment of the recurrence language that
omits
\mathsf{fold}_{\delta}
,
{\Gamma,y\mathbin{:}\rho}\vdash{e^{\prime}}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}
,

{\Gamma}\vdash{e}\mathbin{:}{{F}[{\rho}]}
, and
\eta
is a
\Gamma
-environment,
then

{}\llbracket{\mbox{${F}[({\rho}:{y}).{e^{\prime}},{e}]$}}\rrbracket{\eta}=({}\llbracket{F}\rrbracket{\eta})_{{}\llbracket{\rho}\rrbracket{\eta},{}\llbracket{\sigma}\rrbracket{\eta}}\,({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}a.{}\llbracket{e^{\prime}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{y}\mapsto{a}\}}})\,({}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta}).

Proof 6.5
.

By induction on
F
.

Combining Prop.
10
with
Lemma
6.4
, we conclude that
to define a model of the recurrence language, it suffices to
define an extension of the standard type frame and the following applicative
structure data:

•

The sets
D^{\tau}
, along with an argument that
D^{\tau}
is a complete
upper semi-lattice;

•

The semantic functions for arrow, product, and sum types;

•

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}
for each structure functor
F
.

From this data we can define

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F}(x)=\bigvee\{z\mid\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}\leq x\}
,

F_{\rho,\sigma}
as just given, and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{F,\sigma}

by (
6.1
).
Of course, there are models that are not constructed this way;
Section
7.5
gives an example that is useful for
extracting recurrences for lower bounds.

6.3.
Syntactic sugar

We now introduce some syntactic sugar that will make our discussion of
recurrences somewhat more pleasant. To simplify the discussion, we restrict
the details to the source language type
{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}
and its
recurrence language potential

{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}
,
but we will use analogous notation for other datatypes such as
\mathtt{nat}
and

{\alpha}\,\mathtt{list}
in our examples.
Many of our source-language functions
are really structural folds over some standard datatype—that is, the step
function is a
\mathtt{case}
expression where the argument for each branch is
really the argument to one of the datatype constructors. Accordingly, we
introduce notation for such
\mathtt{fold}
expressions: for

y\notin\mathop{\mathrm{fv}}\nolimits(e_{\mathtt{emp}})\cup\mathop{\mathrm{fv}}\nolimits(e_{\mathtt{node}})
,

\mathtt{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}}\,e\,\mathtt{of}\,{\mathtt{emp}}\Rightarrow{e_{\mathtt{emp}}}\mid{\mathtt{node}}\Rightarrow{(x,r_{0},r_{1}).e_{\mathtt{node}}}

is syntactic sugar for

\mathtt{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}}\,e\,\mathtt{of}\,{w}.{\mathtt{case}\,w\,\mathtt{of}\,{y}.{e_{\mathtt{emp}}};{y}.{{e_{\mathtt{node}}}{\{{\pi_{0}\,y,\pi_{1}\,y,\pi_{2}\,y}/{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\}}}}.

We introduce a similar notation in the recurrence language:

\mathsf{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}\,{e}\,\mathsf{of}\,{\left\{{\mathsf{emp}}\Rightarrow{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\mid{\mathsf{node}}\Rightarrow{(x,r_{0},r_{1}).e_{\mathsf{node}}}\right\}}

is syntactic sugar for

\mathsf{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}\,e\,\mathsf{of}\,{(w:{F_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}}[{\rho}])}.{{(\mathsf{case}\,w\,\mathsf{of}\,{y}.{e_{\mathsf{emp}}};{y}.{{e_{\mathsf{node}}}{\{{\pi_{0}\,y,\pi_{1}\,y,\pi_{2}\,y}/{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\}}})}}

where
w
and
y
are fresh variables.

It would be nice to establish an identity of the form

\|\mathtt{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}}\dotsb\|=\mathsf{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}\dotsb
, but
the size-order axioms, which give us only inequalities, are too weak.
However, the models that we will consider validate many equations, so we can
set out a nice relationship. In the following proposition, we say
“in the semantics,
e=e^{\prime}
” to mean that for any
\eta
,

{}\llbracket{e}\rrbracket{\eta}={}\llbracket{e^{\prime}}\rrbracket{\eta}
:

Proposition 12
.

Suppose that we have a model such that

•

In the semantics: if
{\|e^{\prime}\|}_{c}=0
, then

\|{e}{\{{e^{\prime}}/{x}\}}\|={\|e\|}{\{{{\|e^{\prime}\|}_{p}}/{x}\}}
;
and

•

In the semantics:

{c}+_{c}{\mathsf{case}\,e\,\mathsf{of}\,\{{x}.{e_{i}}\}_{i=0,1}}=\mathsf{case}\,e\,\mathsf{of}\,\{{x}.{{c}+_{c}{e_{i}}}\}_{i=0,1}
.

If
{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathtt{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}}\,e\,\mathtt{of}\,{\mathtt{emp}}\Rightarrow{e_{\mathtt{emp}}}\mid{\mathtt{node}}\Rightarrow{(x,r_{0},r_{1}).e_{\mathtt{node}}}}\mathbin{:}{\rho}
,
then in the semantics,

\|\mathtt{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}}\,e\,\mathtt{of}\,{\mathtt{emp}}\Rightarrow{e_{\mathtt{emp}}}\mid{\mathtt{node}}\Rightarrow{(x,r_{0},r_{1}).e_{\mathtt{node}}}\|=\\
{c}+_{c}{\mathsf{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}\,{p}\,\mathsf{of}\,{\left\{{\mathsf{emp}}\Rightarrow{{1}+_{c}{\|E_{\mathsf{emp}}\|}}\mid{\mathsf{node}}\Rightarrow{(x,r_{0},r_{1}).{{1}+_{c}{\|E_{\mathsf{node}}\|}}}\right\}}}

where
(c,p)=\|e\|
.

While the models that we discuss in subsequent sections satisfy
the hypotheses of Prop.
12
, they are not
necessarily satisfied in an arbitrary model. That requires
additional axioms that correspond roughly to
\eta
axioms.

7.
Examples

7.1.
The standard model

For the standard model, we first extend the standard type frame by including
the constant
\bot
in
\mathbf{U}_{sm}
. A semantic type (scheme) is
proper
if it
has no occurrences of
\bot
. The proper semantic types (type schemes)
correspond exactly to the closed syntactic types (type schemes). In the
definitions of
\mathord{\underline{\smash{\mu}}}
and
\mathord{\underline{\forall}}
, we take
F
and
\tau
to be proper.
We define the sets
A^{\sigma}
by induction on
\sigma

as follows:
5
5

5

The collection of sets
\{A^{\sigma}\}_{{\sigma\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}}}
must be contained
in some set that contains
\emptyset
and a one-element set and is closed under
disjoint unions, products, function spaces, unions of chains, and
products indexed by
\mathbf{U}_{sm}
.
V^{\omega_{1}}
in the standard set-theoretic
hierarchy suffices.

•

A^{\mathsf{C}}=\mathbf{N}
, the natural numbers.

•

A^{\bot}=\emptyset
.

•

A^{\mathsf{unit}}=\{*\}
, some one-element set.

•

A^{{\sigma_{0}}\to{\sigma_{1}}}=(A^{\sigma_{1}})^{A^{\sigma_{0}}}
, the
set of functions from
A^{\sigma_{0}}
to
A^{\sigma_{1}}
.

•

A^{{\sigma_{0}}\times{\sigma_{1}}}=A^{\sigma_{0}}\times A^{\sigma_{1}}
,
where
\times
is the standard set-theoretic product.

•

A^{{\sigma_{0}}+{\sigma_{1}}}=A^{\sigma_{0}}\sqcup A^{\sigma_{1}}
,
where
\sqcup
is the standard set-theoretic disjoint union.

•

A^{\mu{t}.F}=\bigcup_{i}A^{({\lambda\hskip-3.68748pt\lambda}V.{}\llbracket{F}\rrbracket{\{{t}\mapsto{V}\}})^{i}\bot}
.

•

A^{\forall{\alpha}.\tau}=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}}A^{{\tau}{\{{\sigma}/{\alpha}\}}}
.

Define
a\leq_{A^{\sigma}}b
iff
a=b
, and let the semantic functions for
arrows, products, and sums be the identity functions.
The definitions of
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}
,
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F}
, and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{F,\sigma}
are
based on the standard initial-algebra semantics.
Note that we cannot use (
6.1
) because
the
A^{\sigma}
are not complete upper semi-lattices, and hence the
hypotheses of Prop.
10
do not hold, and hence

(
\beta_{\delta\mathsf{fold}}
)
must be verified directly.

At first blush, this model is not particularly interesting. There is no
abstraction of values to sizes and the “order” on costs is the
identity, so the recurrences extracted from source language programs
describe the exact cost of those programs in terms of the argument
values. However, this is a standard model of (predicative)
polymorphism, and so we can hope that parametricity may have some
interesting consequences. Free
theorems
(
Wadler 1989
)
have been used to obtain
relative cost information, and we discuss this further in
Section
9
. Here, we apply parametricity to the
recurrence language and sketch the argument that if
g:{{\alpha}\,\mathtt{list}}\to{{\alpha}\,\mathtt{list}}
, then the cost of
g(xs)
depends
only on the length of
xs
(the same can be said for the length
of
g(xs)
, but this follows from parametricity applied to the
source language). For any
\rho
, let us define
T_{\rho}(xs)=(({}\llbracket{\|g\|}\rrbracket{}\,\langle\!\langle\rho\rangle\!\rangle)_{p}(xs))_{c}
, the exact cost of evaluating

g(xs)
(since
\|\cdot\|
is a monadic translation and the
interpretation of inductive types is the standard one, syntactic values
of list type in the source language are isomorphic to the semantic
values in the model). The goal is to show that if
xs:{\rho}\,\mathtt{list}
and

ys:{\sigma}\,\mathtt{list}
are of the same length, then
T_{\rho}(xs)=T_{\sigma}(ys)
. To do so, we apply parametricity
to
{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\rho.{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}xs.T_{\rho}(xs)\in A^{\forall\rho.{\rho}\,\mathsf{list}\to\mathsf{C}}
. We take
the relational interpretation of
\mathsf{C}
to be equality (so the cost
constants
0
and
+
preserve the relation). Expanding the definition
of parametricity, this means that for any
\rho
and
\sigma
and relation

R\subseteq A^{\langle\!\langle\rho\rangle\!\rangle}\times A^{\langle\!\langle\sigma\rangle\!\rangle}
, for any
xs\in A^{{\langle\!\langle\rho\rangle\!\rangle}\,\mathsf{list}}
and
ys\in A^{{\langle\!\langle\sigma\rangle\!\rangle}\,\mathsf{list}}
, if
{R}\,\mathsf{list}\subseteq A^{{\langle\!\langle\rho\rangle\!\rangle}\,\mathsf{list}}\times A^{{\langle\!\langle\sigma\rangle\!\rangle}\,\mathsf{list}}
holds for
xs

and
ys
, then the relational interpretation of
\mathsf{C}
holds for

T_{\rho}(xs)
and
T_{\sigma}(ys)
. Since the relational interpretation of
the cost type is equality, this would give the result, so it suffices to
show that there is an
R
such that
({R}\,\mathsf{list})(xs,ys)
holds whenever

xs
and
ys
have the same length. However, the standard relational
lifting
{R}\,\mathsf{list}
holds whenever
xs
and
ys
have the same length and

xs_{i}
is related to
ys_{i}
by
R
, so taking
R
to be the total
relation achieves this. We conclude that if
xs
and
ys
have the same
length, then the cost of
g(xs)
and
g(ys)
is the same.

7.2.
Constructor size and height

We now describe a model in which a value
v
of inductive type
\delta
is
interpreted either by the number of
\delta
constructors in
v
(constructor
size) or by the maximum nesting depth of
\delta
-constructors in
v

(constructor height), so that it reflects common size abstractions such as
list length, tree size, and tree height. For example,
in this model, the interpretation of the recurrence extracted from a function
with domain
{\sigma}\,\mathtt{list}
describes the cost in terms of the length of the
argument list.
For concreteness we will define the constructor size model. For the
interpretation of the types, we will need two versions of the natural numbers:

\mathbf{N}_{0}^{\infty}=\{0,1,\dots,\infty\}
for costs, and

\mathbf{N}_{1}^{\infty}=\{1,2,\dots,\infty\}
for sizes of inductive values, which
must be at least
1
because every value contains at least one constructor.

\mathbf{N}_{i}^{\infty}
is ordered by

x\leq_{\mathbf{N}_{i}^{\infty}}y
if
y=\infty
or
x\leq_{\mathbf{N}}y
.
The presence of
\infty
may be perplexing, since all programs in the source
language terminate. However, it is not always possible to give a finite upper
bound on cost or potential in terms of the potential of the argument, because
the notion of potential used in this model may not identify all possible
sources of recursive calls. For example, consider the function

\mathtt{sumtree}
defined in Figure
22
that sums the nodes of
a
{\mathtt{nat}}\,\mathtt{tree}
. The cost and size of
\mathtt{sumtree}\,t
depend on the size
of
t
and the sizes of its labels, whereas in this model, the potential
of
t
only tells us the former. Since
\mathtt{sumtree}
is
definable in our source language, its recurrence can be extracted, and hence
must have a meaning in this model; the only sensible interpretation is one
that maps every tree size to the trivial upper bound of
\infty
for both
cost and potential.

We start by extending the standard type frame with additional small types

\mathbf{N}_{0},\mathbf{N}_{1}\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}
. Then we define the sets
V^{\tau}
,
observing that each
V^{\tau}
is a complete upper semi-lattice. This allows us
to construct a model by just defining
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}
. The sets
V^{\tau}
are
defined as follows:

•

V^{\mathbf{N}_{i}}=\mathbf{N}_{i}^{\infty}
.

•

V^{\mathsf{C}}=\mathbf{N}_{0}^{\infty}
with the standard interpretations for
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{0}}}

and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{+}}}
, where
x\mathbin{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{+}}}}\infty=\infty\mathbin{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{+}}}}x=\infty
.

•

V^{\mathsf{unit}}=\{*\}
.

•

V^{{\sigma_{0}}\to{\sigma_{1}}}=
the set of monotone functions
from
V^{\sigma_{0}}
to
V^{\sigma_{1}}
with the usual pointwise order, taking

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Abs}}}
and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{App}}}
to be the identity functions.

•

V^{{\sigma_{0}}\times{\sigma_{1}}}=V^{\sigma_{0}}\times V^{\sigma_{1}}
with
the usual component-wise order, taking
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Pair}}}
and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Proj}}}
to be the
standard pairing and projection functions.

•

V^{{\sigma_{0}}+{\sigma_{1}}}=\mathcal{O}(V^{\sigma_{0}}\sqcup V^{\sigma_{1}})
, which we define
in Section
7.2.1
.

•

V^{\mu{t}.F}=\mathbf{N}_{1}^{\infty}
. We define
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}
in
Section
7.2.2

(recall that we write

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}
for
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{{\lambda\hskip-3.68748pt\lambda}V.{}\llbracket{F}\rrbracket{\{{V}/{t}\}}}
, etc., and
that we can define
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F}
and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{F,\sigma}
from it).

•

V^{\forall{\alpha}.\tau}=\prod_{\sigma\in\mathbf{U}_{sm}}V^{{\tau}{\{{\sigma}/{\alpha}\}}}
, with the pointwise order,
taking
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyAbs}}}
and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyApp}}}
to be the identity functions.

Once we define the interpretation of sums and datatypes, it is straightforward
to verify that this is a model.

Proposition 13
.

\mathbf{V}=\{V^{\tau}\}_{{\tau\in\mathbf{U}_{lg}}}
is a model of the recurrence
language.

Proof 7.1
.

Since
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Abs}}}
and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{TyAbs}}}
are total, it suffices to verify the conditions
on the semantic functions. This is trivial for arrows, products, and type
quantification; sums and inductive types are handled in the next two sections.

7.2.1.
Interpretation of sums

As we observed, we need to ensure that
all the sets
V^{\sigma}
are complete upper semi-lattices.
Preserving the complete upper semi-lattice property is
straightforward for all type constructors except sum. We could take the usual
disjoint sum along with a new infinite element
\infty
that is a common upper
bound of elements on both sides, but that ends up leading to very weak bounds
in practice. For example, recall that
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}(2)
should tell
us about the data that can be used to construct a list of size
\leq 2

(which is a
\mathsf{cons}
list, because we count the
number of
\mathsf{c}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}
constructors, so
\mathsf{nil}
has
size
1
).
If we were to interpret sums as just proposed,
both
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{0}(*)
and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{1}(a,0)
are such values, and their least
upper bound would be
\infty
. It is not hard to parlay this into an argument
that if
\mathtt{tail}=\lambda{xs}.{\mathtt{case}\,xs\,\mathtt{of}\,{x}.{\mathtt{nil}};{x}.{\pi_{1}\,x}}

is the usual tail function on
{\sigma}\,\mathtt{list}
, then the recurrence extracted
from
\mathtt{tail}
gives a bound of
\infty
for all lists of length
>1
.
While correct, this is hardly satisfying!

Instead, we take inspiration from abstract interpretation

(
Cousot and Cousot 1977
)
: we will define
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F}(n)
to
be the
set

of values
x
such that
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}(x)\leq n
. We can arrange this for the
typical cases of interest (i.e., finitary inductive datatypes such as lists
and trees) by defining
V^{{\sigma_{0}}+{\sigma_{1}}}
to be the
downward closed subsets of
V^{\sigma_{0}}\sqcup V^{\sigma_{1}}
. We could
arrange this for
all

inductive datatypes if we were to do something similar in
the interpretation of arrows and products, but that entails some additional
notational cost in reasoning about extracted recurrences while providing
no benefits for the examples that we present.
We start with some standard order-theoretic and set-theoretic definitions:

•

For any partially ordered set
A
, the
order ideal
of
A
is

\mathcal{O}(A)=_{df}\{X\subseteq A\mid\text{$x\in X$ and $y\leq x$}\Rightarrow y\in X\}.

\mathcal{O}(A)
is partially ordered by set inclusion and is a complete
upper semi-lattice; concretely, if
X\subseteq\mathcal{O}(A)
, then

\bigvee X=\bigcup X
.

•

For any
X\subseteq A
,

\mathop{\downarrow^{A}}\nolimits X=\{x\in A\mid\exists y\in X.x\leq y\}\in\mathcal{O}(A)

and for
a\in A
,
\mathop{\downarrow^{A}}\nolimits a=\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits\{a\}
(we drop the superscript
when it is clear from context).

•

For any
f:A\to B
and
X\subseteq A
,

f[X]=\{f(x)\mid x\in X\}
.

•

If
X_{0}
and
X_{1}

are partially-ordered sets,
X_{0}\sqcup X_{1}

is the usual disjoint union with injection functions

\mathrm{in}^{i}:X_{i}\to X_{0}\sqcup X_{1}
partially ordered by
x\leq y
iff

x=\mathrm{in}^{i}(x^{\prime})
,
y=\mathrm{in}^{i}(y^{\prime})
, and
x^{\prime}\leq_{X_{i}}y^{\prime}
.

For the interpretation of sums, we define
V^{{\sigma_{0}}+{\sigma_{1}}}=\mathcal{O}(V^{\sigma_{0}}\sqcup V^{\sigma_{1}})
with the semantic functions
defined by

\displaystyle\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{i}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{in}^{i}[\mathop{\downarrow^{V^{\sigma_{i}}}}\nolimits x]=\mathop{\downarrow^{V^{\sigma_{0}}\sqcup V^{\sigma_{1}}}}\nolimits(\mathrm{in}^{i}(x))

\displaystyle\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}(X_{0}\sqcup X_{1},f_{0},f_{1})

\displaystyle=\bigvee f_{0}[X_{0}]\vee\bigvee f_{1}[X_{1}]

Lemma 7.2
.

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{i}(x),f_{0},f_{1})\geq f_{i}(x)
.

Proof 7.3
.

\displaystyle\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{i}(x),f_{0},f_{1})

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}(\mathrm{in}^{i}[\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits x]\sqcup\emptyset,f_{0},f_{1})

\displaystyle=\bigvee f_{i}[\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits x]\vee\bigvee f_{1-i}[\emptyset]

\displaystyle=\bigvee f_{i}[\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits x]

\displaystyle\geq f_{i}(x)

\displaystyle(x\in\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits x).

Note that
\mathcal{O}A
is a monad on the category of partially ordered
sets and monotone functions, with unit
A\to\mathcal{O}A
given by

\downarrow^{A}
, and multiplication
\mathcal{O}{\mathcal{O}A}\to\mathcal{O}A
given by union, and it plays the role of a powerset monad
on posets (the ordinary powerset operation, without the additional
downward closure requirement, does not have a
monotone
function

A\to\mathcal{P}(A)
, because
x\leq_{A}y
does not imply that

\{x\}\subseteq\{y\}
.
When a partially ordered set is a complete upper
semilattice (i.e. supports the maximum operation that we use to
interpret the recursor), it is an
algebra
for this monad,
i.e. there is a monotone function
\mathcal{O}A\to A
, satisfying some
equations. Thus, another way of understanding these models is that, for
functions and products, we build algebras
\bigvee_{A\to B}:\mathcal{O}(A\to B)\to(A\to B)
and
\bigvee_{A\times B}:\mathcal{O}(A\times B)\to A\times B
from algebra structures
\bigvee_{A}:\mathcal{O}A\to A
and
\bigvee_{B}:\mathcal{O}B\to B
, but for sums, we use the
free algebra
\mathcal{O}(A+B)
, with
\bigvee_{\mathcal{O}(A+B)}:\mathcal{O}{\mathcal{O}(A+B)}\to\mathcal{O}{(A+B)}
given by union.

7.2.2.
Semantic functions for inductive datatypes

We define
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}
by first defining
a function
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F}:V^{{F}[{\mu{t}.F}]}\to\mathbf{N}_{0}^{\infty}
.
For
\delta=\mu{t}.F
, a semantic value of
type
{{F}[{\mu{t}.F}]}

represents the data from which a value of type
\delta

is constructed, but with the inductive substructures replaced by their sizes,
and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F}
returns the size of the inductive value constructed from that
data.

\begin{aligned} \underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{t}(n)&=n\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{\sigma}(x)&=0\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{{F_{0}}+{F_{1}}}{}(X_{0}\sqcup X_{1})&=\bigvee\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{0}}[X_{0}]\vee\bigvee\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{1}}[X_{1}]\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{{F_{0}}\times{F_{1}}}(a_{0},a_{1})&=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{0}}{}(a_{0})+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{1}}(a_{1})\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{{\sigma}\to{F}}(g)&=\sum\{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F}(g\,x)\mid x\in V^{\sigma}\}\end{aligned}

For the constructor height model, define a function
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{height}}}_{F}

analogously, replacing the sums in the
product and arrow shapes with maximums.
The semantic constructor and destructor are then defined by

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}(a)=1+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F}(a)\qquad\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F}(n)=\bigvee\{a\mid\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}\,a\leq n\}.

To use (
6.1
), it suffices to verify
the conditions of
Prop.
10
, which is trivial, so we have

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{F,\sigma}\,s\,x=\bigvee\{s\bigl(F_{\delta,\sigma}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{F,\sigma}\,s)z\bigr)\mid 1+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F}(z)\leq x\}.

7.2.3.
Examples: lists and trees

Referring to Figure
10
,

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\underline{~}}\vdash{\mathsf{nil}}\mathbin{:}{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${x\mathbin{:}\sigma,xs\mathbin{:}{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}\vdash{\mathsf{cons}(x,xs)}\mathbin{:}{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=1+\eta(xs)

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\underline{~}}\vdash{\mathsf{emp}}\mathbin{:}{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mbox{${x\mathbin{:}\sigma,t_{0}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree},t_{1}\mathbin{:}{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}\vdash{\mathsf{node}(x,t_{0},t_{1})}\mathbin{:}{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}$}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle=1+\eta(t_{0})+\eta(t_{1}).

It is not hard to see that

{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\underline{~}}\vdash{t}\mathbin{:}{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}$}}\rrbracket{}=2n+1
, where
n
is the usual size
of
t
(i.e.,
{}\llbracket{\mbox{${\underline{~}}\vdash{t}\mathbin{:}{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}$}}\rrbracket{}
is
the number of internal and external nodes of
t
).
Since this is linear in the usual
notion of size of a tree, it suffices for showing that the recurrences that we
extract have the expected
O
-behavior.

Destructors exhibit the desired behavior; consider
{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}
again:

\displaystyle\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}}(x)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\{*\}\sqcup\emptyset,&x=1\\
\{*\}\sqcup\{(a,x^{\prime})\mid a\in V^{\sigma},1+x^{\prime}\leq x\},&2\leq x\leq\infty\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\{*\}\sqcup(V^{\sigma}\times\mathop{\downarrow^{\mathbf{N}_{1}^{\infty}}}\nolimits(x-1))

where we define
\mathop{\downarrow^{\mathbf{N}_{1}^{\infty}}}\nolimits 0=\emptyset
.
In other words,
a list of size
1
must be
\mathtt{nil}

and a list of length at most
x
is either
\mathtt{nil}

or
\mathtt{cons}(x,xs)
, where
xs
has length at most
x-1
.
Remember that
V^{{F}[{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}]}=\mathcal{O}(\{*\}\sqcup(V^{\sigma}\times\mathbf{N}_{1}^{\infty}))
, so if

\mathrm{in}^{1}(a,x)\in X\in V^{{F}[{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}]}
, then
x\geq 1
; that is
why
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}}(1)\not=\{*\}\sqcup X
with

X\not=\emptyset
.
For

{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}
, the result is equally pleasant:

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}}(x)=\begin{cases}\{*\}\sqcup\emptyset,&x=1\\
\{*\}\sqcup\{(a,x_{0},x_{1})\mid a\in V^{\sigma},1+x_{0}+x_{1}\leq x\},&2\leq x\leq\infty\end{cases}

Finally, we observe the following simple forms for the denotation of
recurrences over lists and trees:

Proposition 14
.

(1)

If

f\,n={}\llbracket{\mathsf{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}\,{y}\,\mathsf{of}\,{\left\{{\mathsf{nil}}\Rightarrow{e_{\mathsf{nil}}}\mid{\mathsf{cons}}\Rightarrow{(x,r).e_{\mathsf{cons}}}\right\}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{y}\mapsto{n}\}}}
,
then in the constructor size and height models,

\displaystyle f\,1

\displaystyle={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{nil}}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle f\,n

\displaystyle={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{nil}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee\{{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{cons}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r}\mapsto{\infty^{\sigma},f\,n^{\prime}}\}}}\mid n^{\prime}<n\}

\displaystyle={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{nil}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{cons}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r}\mapsto{\infty^{\sigma},f(n-1)}\}}}

\displaystyle(n>1).

The second form for
f\,n
,
n>1
, follows from monotonicity of the denotation
function.

(2)

If

f\,n={}\llbracket{\mathsf{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}\,{y}\,\mathsf{of}\,{\left\{{\mathsf{emp}}\Rightarrow{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\mid{\mathsf{node}}\Rightarrow{(x,r_{0},r_{1}).e_{\mathsf{node}}}\right\}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{y}\mapsto{n}\}}}
,
then in the constructor size model,

\displaystyle f\,1

\displaystyle={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}

\displaystyle f\,n

\displaystyle={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee\{{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\mapsto{\infty^{\sigma},f\,n_{0},f\,n_{1}}\}}}\mid n_{0}+n_{1}<n\}

\displaystyle(n>1).

In the constructor height model, replace
n_{0}+n_{1}<n
with

n_{0}\vee n_{1}<n
.

Proof 7.4
.

The verification is a moderately tedious calculation; here it is
for (2) with
n>1
. Let

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}(Z\sqcup X).{}\llbracket{{\mathsf{case}\,w\,\mathsf{of}\,{y}.{e_{\mathsf{emp}}};{y}.{{e_{\mathsf{node}}}{\{{\pi_{0}\,y,\pi_{1}\,y,\pi_{2}\,y}/{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\}}}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{w}\mapsto{Z\sqcup X}\}}}

\displaystyle={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}(Z\sqcup X).{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee\bigl\{{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{a,b_{0},b_{1}}\mapsto{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\}}}\mid(a,b_{0},b_{1})\in X\bigr\}

Observe that
f=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}\,s
and let us write

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Map}}}
for

({}\llbracket{F_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}}\rrbracket{\eta})_{{}\llbracket{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}\rrbracket{\eta},{}\llbracket{\rho}\rrbracket{\eta}}
.
By definition, we have

f\,n=\bigvee\{s(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Map}}}\,f\,(Z\sqcup X))\mid\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}(Z\sqcup X)\leq n\}
.
By monotonicity we need only consider
Z=\{*\}
, and by definition
of
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}
we need only consider non-empty sets
X
such that

(\underline{~},n_{0},n_{1})\in X
implies
n_{0}+n_{1}<n
, so

\displaystyle f\,n

\displaystyle=\bigvee\{s(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Map}}}\,f\,(\{*\}\sqcup X))\mid(\underline{~},n_{0},n_{1})\in X\Rightarrow n_{0}+n_{1}<n\}

\displaystyle=\bigvee\{s(\{*\}\sqcup\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Map}}}\,f\,(\mathrm{in}^{1}[X]))\mid(\underline{~},n_{0},n_{1})\in X\Rightarrow n_{0}+n_{1}<n\}

\displaystyle=\bigvee\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee\bigl\{{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\mapsto{b,k_{0},k_{1}}\}}}\mid(b,k_{0},k_{1})\in\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Map}}}\,f\,(\mathrm{in}^{1}[X])\bigr\}\\
&\mid(\underline{~},n_{0},n_{1})\in X\Rightarrow n_{0}+n_{1}<n\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&\bigvee\bigl\{{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\mapsto{b,k_{0},k_{1}}\}}}\mid(b,k_{0},k_{1})\in\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Map}}}\,f\,(\mathrm{in}^{1}[X])\bigr\}\\
&\mid(\underline{~},n_{0},n_{1})\in X\Rightarrow n_{0}+n_{1}<n\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&\bigvee\begin{aligned} \bigl\{&{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\mapsto{b,k_{0},k_{1}}\}}}\\
&\mid(b,k_{0},k_{1})\in\bigvee\{\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits(a,f\,n_{0},f\,n_{1})\mid(a,n_{0},n_{1})\in X\}\bigr\}\end{aligned}\\
&\mid(\underline{~},n_{0},n_{1})\in X\Rightarrow n_{0}+n_{1}<n\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&\bigvee\begin{aligned} \bigl\{&{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\mapsto{b,k_{0},k_{1}}\}}}\\
&\mid\exists(a,n_{0},n_{1})\in X:(b,k_{0},k_{1})\leq(a,f\,n_{0},f\,n_{1})\bigr\}\end{aligned}\\
&\mid(\underline{~},n_{0},n_{1})\in X\Rightarrow n_{0}+n_{1}<n\Bigr\}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee\bigl\{{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\mapsto{\infty^{\sigma},f\,n_{0},f\,n_{1}}\}}}\mid n_{0}+n_{1}<n\bigr\}

Let us write the last equation as

L={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee\Bigl\{\bigvee A_{X}\mid(\underline{~},n_{0},n_{1})\in X\Rightarrow n_{0}+n_{1}<n\Bigr\}={}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{emp}}}\rrbracket{\eta}\vee\bigvee B=R

First let us show that for any
X
such that

(\underline{~},n_{0},n_{1})\in X\Rightarrow n_{0}+n_{1}<n
,
A_{X}\subseteq\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits B
, from
which we conclude that
\bigvee A_{X}\leq\bigvee B
, and hence

L\leq R
. For any such
X
, take
(b,k_{0},k_{1})
such that there is

(a,n_{0},n_{1})\in X
with
(b,k_{0},k_{1})\leq(a,f\,n_{0},f\,n_{1})\leq(\infty,f\,n_{0},f\,n_{1})
.
By Prop.
13
,

{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\mapsto{b,k_{0},k_{1}}\}}}\leq{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\mapsto{\infty,f\,n_{0},f\,n_{1}}\}}}
. Since

(b,k_{0},k_{1})
was chosen arbitrarily,
A_{X}\subseteq\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits B
, as needed.
To show that
R\leq L
, suppose that
n_{0}+n_{1}<n
. Then

{}\llbracket{e_{\mathsf{node}}}\rrbracket{{\eta}{\{{x,r_{0},r_{1}}\mapsto{\infty,f\,n_{0},f\,n_{1}}\}}}\in A_{\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits(\infty,n_{0},n_{1})}
, from which
R\leq L
follows.

Although we will primarily use Prop.
14
,
it may be instructive to work through an example of explicitly constructing

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Fold}}}_{F,\rho}
from the proof
of Prop.
10
.
Consider
F={\mathsf{unit}}+{t}
(the structure functor
for
\mathsf{nat}
), and set

s(x)=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Case}}}(x,{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}u.1,{\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}u.1+x)
.
s
might be the step
function for the recurrence that describes the cost of the
copy function on
\mathtt{nat}
. Define
Q
as in the proof of
Lemma
10
. In this setting, the bottom element at which
we start iterating
Q
is the function that is constantly
0
. Set

f_{0}=Q\,\bot
and
f_{n+1}=Q\,f_{n}
. Just as in the calculation
of
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{list}}}
,

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}(x)=\begin{cases}\{*\}\sqcup\emptyset,&x=1\\
\{*\}\sqcup\{x^{\prime}\mid 1+x^{\prime}\leq x\},&2\leq x\end{cases}

and so a bit more calculation shows that

f_{k}(n)=\begin{cases}n,&n\leq k+1\\
k+1,&n>k+1.\end{cases}

It is not hard to see that

f_{\omega}(n)=n
is a fixed point of
Q
, so we conclude that

{}\llbracket{\mathsf{fold}_{\mathsf{nat}}\,{x}\,\mathsf{of}\,{\left\{{\mathsf{Z}}\Rightarrow{1}\mid{\mathsf{S}}\Rightarrow{r.\mathsf{S}r}\right\}}}\rrbracket{\{{x}\mapsto{n}\}}=n
.

Of course, this is precisely
what we expect, though for readers familiar with how a typical recursive
function on numbers is defined by successive approximations, the route may
feel a bit different. Usually when defining a recursive function on numbers,
one takes the flat order and starts with the everywhere-undefined function.
For a typical total function, the
k
-th
approximation is a partial function that is defined and correct
on some initial segment of the natural numbers
and undefined elsewhere. Here we take the (more-or-less) standard order
and start with a function
that is everywhere an unlikely bound (namely,
0
). Each successive
approximation yields a function with more likely bounds,
terminating with a (hopefully low but) correct bound. In the case of a
partial recursive function, the “bad” case is that the function is not
defined for some numbers (the value of the approximants never gets
above
\bot
). In our setting, the “bad” case is that the bound is infinite
(the value of the approximants never stops growing).
The reader may wish to compare this with the use of
\mathbf{N}_{0}^{\infty}
by

Rosendahl 1989
, where
\infty
corresponds to the
bottom element in the usual CPO semantics for fixpoints. We return to this in
Section
8
when we discuss general recursion.

7.2.4.
Example: tree copy

\displaystyle\mathtt{copy}_{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}}

\displaystyle=\lambda{t}.{\mathtt{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}}\,t\,\mathtt{of}\,\begin{aligned} &\phantom{{}\mid{}}{\mathtt{emp}}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{emp}}\\
&{}\mid{\mathtt{node}}\Rightarrow{(x,r_{0},r_{1}).\mathtt{node}(x,\mathtt{force}\,r_{0},\mathtt{force}\,r_{1}).}\end{aligned}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{copy}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}

\displaystyle=\lambda{t}.{\mathsf{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}\,{t}\,\mathsf{of}\,{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\phantom{{}\mid{}}{\mathsf{emp}}\Rightarrow{(1,1)}\\
&{}\mid{\mathsf{node}}\Rightarrow{(x,r_{0},r_{1}).(1+{r_{0}}_{c}+{r_{1}}_{c},\mathsf{node}(x,{r_{0}}_{p},{r_{1}}_{p}))}\end{aligned}\right\}}}.

Figure 20
.
The monomorphic tree copy function and its extracted recurrence.

For a first “sanity check,” let us analyze the tree copy function that is
defined in Figure
20
. We will also describe
some of the
main features in the analysis that are typical of all of our examples.
The first is that a source language program

e=\lambda{x,y,z}.{e^{\prime}}
extracts to a recurrence of the form

(0,\lambda{x}.{(0,\lambda{y}.{(0,\lambda{z}.{\|e^{\prime}\|})})})
.
However, we are really only interested in
\|e^{\prime}\|
as a function
of the potentials
x
,
y
, and
z
. Accordingly, when analyzing a
program such as
e
, we focus on the recurrence language
program
\lambda{x,y,z}.{\|e^{\prime}\|}
. Here, this means we will analyze
the (denotation of the)
recurrence
\mathsf{copy}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}
that is also shown
in Figure
20
.
Second, we shall use
Prop.
12
freely as though it is a theorem
about the syntax when we write our examples. Third,
in our examples, we typically
use the identifier
r
in syntactic recurrences for a recursive call to the
computation of a complexity, and hence
{r}_{p}
and
{r}_{c}
correspond to
recursive calls that compute potential and cost, respectively.
Finally, we remind
the reader that our goal is to show that the semantic recurrences are
essentially the same as those that we expect to arise from an informal
analysis, and so we make no attempt to solve them.

The analysis for
\mathtt{copy}_{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}}
proceeds as follows.
Define

T(n)=({}\llbracket{\mathsf{copy}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}}\rrbracket{}(n))_{c}
. Following the definition
of the denotation function and using Prop.
14
and
facts about
\vee
and
\bigvee
in the semantics, we have

T(1)=1\qquad\begin{aligned} T(n)&=1\vee\bigvee\{1+T(n_{0})+T(n_{1})\mid n_{0}+n_{1}<n\}\\
&=\bigvee\{1+T(n_{0})+T(n_{1})\mid n_{0}+n_{1}<n\}\end{aligned}

and we obtain a similar recurrence for

S(n)=({}\llbracket{\mathsf{copy}_{{\sigma}\,\mathsf{tree}}}\rrbracket{}(n))_{p}
. We observe that these are
precisely the expected recurrences from an informal analysis which, if one is
careful, must consider all possible combinations of subtree sizes when
computing the cost or size of the result when the argument tree has size
n
.

7.2.5.
Example: binary search tree membership

\displaystyle\mathtt{mem}={}

\displaystyle\lambda{cmp^{{{\sigma}\times{\sigma}}\to{\mathtt{order}}},t^{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}},x^{\sigma}}.

\displaystyle{\mathtt{fold}_{{\sigma}\,\mathtt{tree}}\,t\,\mathtt{of}\,\begin{aligned} &\phantom{{}\mid{}}{\mathtt{emp}}\Rightarrow{\mathtt{false}}\\
&{}\mid{\mathtt{node}}\Rightarrow{(y,r_{0},r_{1}).\begin{aligned} \mathtt{case}\,{cmp}\,{(x,y)}\,\mathtt{of}\,&\mathtt{LT}.{\mathtt{force}\,r_{0}};\\
&\mathtt{EQ}.{\mathtt{true}};\\
&\mathtt{GT}.{\mathtt{force}\,r_{1}}\end{aligned}}\end{aligned}}

\mathsf{mem}=\\
\begin{aligned} &\lambda{(cmp:{{\langle\!\langle\sigma\rangle\!\rangle}\times{\langle\!\langle\sigma\rangle\!\rangle}}\to{\|\mathsf{order}\|}),(h:{\langle\!\langle\sigma\rangle\!\rangle}\,\mathsf{tree}),(x:\langle\!\langle\sigma\rangle\!\rangle)}.\\
&\mathsf{fold}_{{\langle\!\langle\sigma\rangle\!\rangle}\,\mathsf{tree}}\,{h}\,\mathsf{of}\,{\left\{\begin{aligned} &\phantom{{}\mid{}}{\mathsf{emp}}\Rightarrow{(1,\mathsf{false})}\\
&{}\mid{\mathsf{node}}\Rightarrow{(y,r_{0},r_{1}).\begin{aligned} \mathsf{case}\,{({cmp}\,{(x,y)})}_{p}\,\mathsf{of}\,&\mathsf{LT}.{(1+{({cmp}\,{(x,y)})}_{c}+{r_{0}}_{c},{r_{0}}_{p})};\\
&\mathsf{EQ}.{(1,\mathsf{true})};\\
&\mathsf{GT}.{(1+{({cmp}\,{(x,y)})}_{c}+{r_{1}}_{c},{r_{1}}_{p})}\end{aligned}}\end{aligned}\right\}}\end{aligned}

Figure 21
.
Binary search tree membership and its extracted recurrence.

For an interesting example, let us consider membership testing in

\sigma
-labeled binary search trees. First we define the type

\mathtt{order}={{\mathtt{unit}}+{\mathtt{unit}}}+{\mathtt{unit}}

and write
\mathtt{case}\,e\,\mathtt{of}\,\mathtt{LT}.{e_{0}};\mathtt{EQ}.{e_{1}};\mathtt{GT}.{e_{2}}
for

\mathtt{case}^{\mathtt{order}}\,e\,\mathtt{of}\,x.e_{0};x.e_{1};x.e_{2}
, and we assume
comparable notation in the recurrence language. The membership test function
is given in Figure
21
.

Let us consider
an informal analysis of
\mathtt{mem}
, which is somewhat simpler to describe in
reference to the
\mathtt{member}
function of
Figure
8
(a). Let
T(h)
be
the number of calls to

\mathtt{member}
in terms of the height of
t
. We would probably argue that

T(1)=1
and for
h>1
,

T(h)\leq\underbrace{1}_{\text{the call to $\mathtt{member}$}}+\underbrace{\bigvee\{0,T(h_{0}),T(h_{1})\}}_{\text{cost of a $\mathtt{case}$ is
bounded by the costs of its branches}},

where

h_{0},h_{1}<h
. But since the only information we have is that
t_{0}
and

t_{1}
are subtrees of some tree
t
of height
h
, what we must
really mean is that

T(h)\leq 1+\bigvee\{0,T(h_{0}),T(h_{1})\mid h_{0}\vee h_{1}<h\},

so this is the recurrence we expect to see in a formal analysis.

Taking the same approach as in the previous section, we analyze the
recurrence
\mathsf{mem}
given in Figure
21
,
this time considering its denotation in
the constructor height model.
The extracted recurrence makes explicit the dependence of the complexity of

\mathtt{mem}
on the complexity of the comparison function
cmp
. Of course, a
typical analysis will make assumptions about this complexity. The most
common such (and the one we implicitly made in our informal analysis)
is that the cost of the comparison function is independent of the
size of its arguments, which we can model here by assuming that

{({cmp}\,{(x,y)})}_{c}=0

for all
x
and
y
(more precisely, we only analyze
{}\llbracket{\mathsf{mem}}\rrbracket{}\,cmp

under
the assumption that
cmp
satisfies this condition).
Define

T(h)=({}\llbracket{\mathsf{mem}}\rrbracket{\,}cmp\,h\,x)_{c}
and assume that

cmp(x,\infty)=A\sqcup B\sqcup C
. Then making use
of Prop.
14
,

T(1)=1\qquad\begin{aligned} T(h)&=1\vee\bigvee\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&\begin{aligned} &\bigvee\{1+{(mem\,cmp\,h_{0}\,x)}_{c}\mid a\in A\}\vee\\
&\quad\bigvee\{1\mid b\in B\}\vee\\
&\quad\bigvee\{1+{(mem\,cmp\,h_{1}\,x)}_{c}\mid c\in C\}\end{aligned}\\
&\mid h_{0}\vee h_{1}<h\Bigr\}\end{aligned}\\
&=1\vee\bigvee\begin{aligned} \Bigl\{&\begin{aligned} &\bigvee\{1+T(h_{0})\mid a\in A\}\vee\\
&\quad\bigvee\{1\mid b\in B\}\vee\\
&\quad\bigvee\{1+T(h_{1})\mid c\in C\}\end{aligned}\\
&\mid h_{0}\vee h_{1}<h\Bigr\}\end{aligned}\\
&\leq 1\vee\bigvee\{1+T(h_{0}),1,1+T(h_{1})\mid h_{0}\vee h_{1}<h\}\\
&=\bigvee\{1+T(h_{0}),1,1+T(h_{1})\mid h_{0}\vee h_{1}<h\}.\end{aligned}

Again, we have essentially the same recurrence as given by the informal
analysis.
The last inequality is valid because
A
,
B
, and
C
are all subsets
of
\{*\}
, and hence are either
\emptyset
or
\{*\}
itself, and we
take advantage of the fact that
\bigvee\emptyset=0
.
The comparison
with
\infty
might be a bit perturbing. In this
model, the labels do not contribute to the potential of a tree. Since the
comparison in the recurrence arises from the comparison of
x
with an
arbitrary node label
y
, the best we can say about the potential of
y
is
that it is at most
\infty
.
For another perspective, keep in mind that
cmp
is monotone, so unless it is
a particularly odd function,

cmp(x,\infty)=\{*\}\sqcup\{*\}\sqcup\{*\}
, which forces the
recurrence to take all possible outcomes into account. This is precisely what
we would expect in an informal analysis.

7.2.6.
Inductive types as an abstract interpretation

Our justification for the interpretation of sum types appealed to intuition
from abstract interpretation. For datatypes
with structure functors that are sums of products (e.g., lists and trees), the
connection goes beyond just intuition, as it is easy to see that
not only do we have that

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}\mathbin{\circ}\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}\geq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

(
\beta_{\delta}
)
but also
that
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}\mathbin{\circ}\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
. This is precisely the kind of
Galois connection we would expect to see in an abstract interpretation, where
here we think of the datatype as being the abstract domain and its unfolding
to be the concrete domain.
6
6

6

Of course, the domains here are not of finite height as in typical AI
analyses, but that is typically for the benefit of computability of those
analyses; that would correspond to computing the denotation of the bounding
recurrence, which is not our primary concern here.

Intuitively, this is exactly how we think of
models of the recurrence language as performing a size abstraction on
datatypes. Interpreting a datatype value (i.e., an application of the
constructor) as a size abstracts away information. Destructing a size
tells us how a value of that size may be constructed from other data, but that
data can only tell us the sizes of the substructures used in the construction.
In other words, the application of the destructor gives us more concrete
information about a size, namely, something about the composition of a value
of that size.

7.3.
Counting all constructors

The cost of
some functions cannot be usefully described in terms of the “usual” notion of
size captured by the model
\mathbf{V}
of the previous section. For example, to
usefully analyze the
\mathtt{sumtree}
function of Figure
22
, we
need a model in which the size of a
{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}
value measures both the
number of
{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}
constructors and the number of
\mathsf{nat}
constructors.
In this section, we give an example of how to construct such a model. In it,
a value
v
of inductive type is interpreted by a function
\phi
such that

\phi(\delta)

is the size of the largest maximal subtree of
v

that contains only
\delta
-constructors.
For
v:{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}
, this means that

{}\llbracket{v}\rrbracket{}({\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree})
is the usual size of
v
,
{}\llbracket{v}\rrbracket{}(\mathsf{nat})
is
the maximum label size
of
v
, and
{}\llbracket{v}\rrbracket{}(\delta)=0
for
\delta\notin\{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree},\mathsf{nat}\}
.

Because we want to distinguish between constructors for different inductive
types,
it is convenient to use the following alternative
grammar for types and structure functors, which just spells out the closed
type production for structure functors:

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle::=\alpha\mid\mathsf{C}\mid\mathsf{unit}\mid{\sigma}+{\sigma}\mid{\sigma}\times{\sigma}\mid{\sigma}\to{\sigma}\mid\mu{t}.F

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle::=t\mid\alpha\mid\mathsf{C}\mid\mathsf{unit}\mid\mu{t}.F\mid{F}+{F}\mid{F}\times{F}\mid{\sigma}\to{F}.

The content of the next proposition is just that the grammar (
*
) defines
the same words as that of Figure
9
.

Proposition 15
.

(1)

If
\sigma
is a type by the grammar (
*
), then
\sigma
is a structure
functor by the grammar (
*
).

(2)

\sigma
is a type by the grammar of Figure
9

iff
\sigma
is a type by the grammar (
*
), and
F
is a structure functor
by the grammar of Figure
9
iff
F
is a structure
functor by the grammar (
*
).

Proof 7.5
.

(1)

Induction on
\sigma
.

(2)

Induction on the
\mu
-nesting depth of
\sigma
and
F
.
The main idea is that we treat
t
as a fixed symbol, rather than a
meta-variable ranging over a class of variables, so inside the
\mu t.F
production of
F
, it is no longer possible to refer to the
“outer”
t
, and the
\mu t.F
production of
F
always corresponds
to a constant shape functor.

The type frame is the same as for the constructor-counting model
of Section
7.2
; for the current model,
we write
W^{\sigma}
for the interpretation of
\sigma
. Except for
inductive types, the clauses for
W^{\sigma}
are the same as those
for
V^{\sigma}
from
Section
7.2
. Set

\mathcal{D}=\{\mu{t}.F\mid\text{$F$ closed}\}
and

•

W^{\mu{t}.F}=\{\phi\in D\to\mathbf{N}_{0}^{\infty}\mid\phi(\mu{t}.F)\geq 1,\text{$\delta$ not a syntactic subtype of $F$}\Rightarrow\phi(\delta)=0\}
.

To define
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}
and
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F}
, we define

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F,\delta}:W^{{F}[{\delta}]}\to(\mathcal{D}\to\mathbf{N}_{0}^{\infty})

similarly to the previous section. The additional subscript
enables us to track which datatype is the “main” datatype, as the counting
is different for products for the main datatype and others.
The definition is as
follows:

\begin{aligned} \underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{t,\delta}(\phi)&=\phi\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{\mathsf{C},\delta}(x)&={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\delta.0\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{\mathsf{unit},\delta}(*)&={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\delta.0\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{\mu{t}.F,\delta}(\phi)&=\phi\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{{F_{0}}+{F_{1}},\delta}(X_{0}\sqcup X_{1})&=\bigvee\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{0},\delta}[X_{0}]\vee\bigvee\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{1},\delta}[X_{1}]\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{{F_{0}}\times{F_{1}},\delta}(x_{0},x_{1})&={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\delta^{\prime}.\begin{cases}\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{0},\delta}(x_{0})(\delta^{\prime})+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{1},\delta}(x_{1})(\delta^{\prime}),&\delta^{\prime}=\delta\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{0},\delta}(x_{0})(\delta^{\prime})\vee\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{1},\delta}(x_{1})(\delta^{\prime}),&\delta^{\prime}\not=\delta\\
\end{cases}\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{{\sigma}\to{F},\delta}(f)&={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\delta^{\prime}.\begin{cases}\sum\{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F,\delta}(f\,x)(\delta^{\prime})\mid x\in D^{\sigma}\},&\delta^{\prime}=\delta\\
\bigvee\{\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F,\delta}(f\,x)(\delta^{\prime})\mid x\in D^{\sigma}\},&\delta^{\prime}\not=\delta\\
\end{cases}\end{aligned}

Set

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}(a)={\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\delta.\chi_{F}(\delta)+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F,\mu{t}.F}(a)(\delta)\qquad\underline{\smash{\mathrm{D}}}_{F}(\phi)=\bigvee\{a\mid\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}(a)\leq\phi\}

where
\chi_{F}(\delta)=1
if
\delta=\mu{t}.F
,
\chi_{F}(\delta)=0

otherwise. Notice that for
\delta=\mu{t}.F
,

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}(a)(\delta)=1+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F,\delta}(a)(\delta)\geq 1
, so

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F}(a)\in W^{\delta}
.
\mathsf{fold}_{\delta}
is
interpreted by (
6.1
) as usual.

7.3.1.
Example: the potential of
{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}

Although we could prove a general theorem to show that
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F,\delta}

encapsulates the description given above, seeing the details of the specific
case of
{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}
is more illuminating.
To start, some notation is helpful:
set
\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n}\in W^{\mathsf{nat}}
and
\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n,k}\in W^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}

to be the functions

\begin{aligned} \phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n}(\mathsf{nat})&=n\\
\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n}(\underline{~})&=0\end{aligned}\qquad\begin{aligned} \phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n,k}(\mathsf{nat})&=n\\
\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n,k}({{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}})&=k\\
\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n,k}(\underline{~})&=0\end{aligned}

First we start with a useful lemma:

Lemma 7.6
.

{}\llbracket{\mathsf{S}}\rrbracket{}(\phi)=\chi_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}+\phi
, and in particular,

{}\llbracket{\mathsf{S}}\rrbracket{}(\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{k})=\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{k+1}
.

Proof 7.7
.

{}\llbracket{\mathsf{S}}\rrbracket{}(\phi)=\chi_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{\mathsf{nat}},\mathsf{nat}}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{1}(\phi))=\chi_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{\mathsf{nat}},\mathsf{nat}}(\emptyset\sqcup\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits\phi)=\\
\chi_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}+(\bigvee\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{t,\mathsf{nat}}[\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits\phi])=\chi_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}+(\bigvee(\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits\phi))=\chi_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}+\phi.

Now set

\mathsf{n}=\mathsf{S}(\dotsc(\mathsf{S}\,\mathsf{Z})\dotsc)\mathbin{:}\mathsf{nat}

(
n

\mathsf{S}
s).
We will show that
{}\llbracket{\mathsf{n}}\rrbracket{}=\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n+1}

by induction on
n
. For
n=0
,

{}\llbracket{\mathsf{0}}\rrbracket{}=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{0}*)=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}(\{*\}\sqcup\emptyset)=\\
\chi_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{\mathsf{nat}},\mathsf{nat}}(\{*\}\sqcup\emptyset)=\chi_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}+({\lambda\hskip-4.49997pt\lambda}\delta.0)=\chi_{F_{\mathsf{nat}}}=\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{1}.

And for
n\geq 0
, we use Lemma
7.6
to show that

{}\llbracket{\mathsf{n+1}}\rrbracket{}=({}\llbracket{\mathsf{S}}\rrbracket{}\,{}\llbracket{\mathsf{n}}\rrbracket{})={}\llbracket{\mathsf{S}}\rrbracket{}(\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n+1})=\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n+2}.

Now let us consider closed
{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}
expressions built up using only

{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}
and
\mathsf{nat}
constructors—i.e.,
\mathsf{nat}
-labeled binary trees.
We show that if
t
is such a tree, then

{}\llbracket{t}\rrbracket{}=\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{m,k}
, where

m=\bigvee\{1+n\mid\text{$n$ a label in $t$}\}
and
k
is the number of

{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}
constructors in
t
.
In the following calculations, we will save a bit of space by writing

\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F}
for
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F,{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}
.
For
\mathsf{emp}
, the argument is essentially the same as for the analysis
of
{}\llbracket{\mathsf{0}}\rrbracket{}
, noting that

\bigvee\{1+n\mid\text{$n$ a label in $\mathsf{emp}$}\}=\bigvee\emptyset=0
.
For the inductive step, assume that

{}\llbracket{t_{i}}\rrbracket{}=\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n_{i},k_{i}}
, so our goal is to show that

{}\llbracket{\mathsf{node}(\mathsf{n},t_{0},t_{1})}\rrbracket{}=\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{(n+1)\vee n_{0}\vee n_{1},1+k_{0}+k_{1}}
:

\displaystyle{}\llbracket{\mathsf{node}(\mathsf{n},t_{0},t_{1})}\rrbracket{}

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F_{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}}(\underline{\smash{\mathrm{Inj}}}^{1}({}\llbracket{\mathsf{n}}\rrbracket{},{}\llbracket{t_{0}}\rrbracket{},{}\llbracket{t_{1}}\rrbracket{}))

\displaystyle=\underline{\smash{\mathrm{C}}}_{F_{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}}(\emptyset\sqcup\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits(\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n+1},\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n_{0},k_{0}},\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n_{1},k_{1}}))

\displaystyle=\chi_{F_{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}}+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{F_{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}}(\emptyset\sqcup\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits(\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n+1},\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n_{0},k_{0}},\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n_{1},k_{1}}))

\displaystyle=\chi_{F_{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}}+\bigvee\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{{\mathsf{nat}}\times{{t}\times{t}}}[\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits(\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n+1},\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n_{0},k_{0}},\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n_{1},k_{1}})].

If
(\phi^{\prime},\phi_{0}^{\prime},\phi_{1}^{\prime})\in\mathop{\downarrow}\nolimits(\phi^{\mathsf{nat}}_{n+1},\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n_{0},k_{0}},\phi^{{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}_{n_{1},k_{1}})
, then

\displaystyle\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{{{\mathsf{nat}}\times{{t}\times{t}}}}(\phi^{\prime},\phi_{0}^{\prime},\phi_{1}^{\prime})(\delta)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{\mathsf{nat}}(\phi^{\prime})(\delta)+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{t}(\phi_{0}^{\prime})(\delta)+\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{t}(\phi_{1}^{\prime})(\delta),&\delta={{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}\\
\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{\mathsf{nat}}(\phi^{\prime})(\delta)\vee\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{t}(\phi_{0}^{\prime})(\delta)\vee\underline{\smash{\mathrm{size}}}_{t}(\phi_{1}^{\prime})(\delta),&\delta\not={{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\phi^{\prime}({{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}})+\phi_{0}^{\prime}({{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}})+\phi_{1}^{\prime}({{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}),&\delta={{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}\\
\phi^{\prime}(\delta)\vee\phi_{0}^{\prime}(\delta)\vee\phi_{1}^{\prime}(\delta),&\delta\not={{\mathsf{nat}}\,\mathsf{tree}}\end{cases}

and hence the computation of

{}\llbracket{\mathsf{node}(\mathsf{n},t_{0},t_{1})}\rrbracket{}(\delta)
proceeds as:
